\section{Two balance laboratories: what this book actually
reproduces}\label{two-balance-laboratories-what-this-book-actually-reproduces}

\subsection{A promise with a boundary}\label{a-promise-with-a-boundary}

Imagine standing on a basketball. Moving your feet changes the ball's
motion; the ball's motion changes the surface under your feet. Now
imagine stepping onto a narrow plank suspended by four cables. Every
footstep changes the support that the next footstep depends on. These
are \textbf{coupled balance problems}, not simply walking animations
placed on unusual scenery.

This book follows the working Microduck repository from physical
assumptions to simulation, observations, actions, PPO updates, exported
policies, evaluation, and the seven-case Duck Viewer. It assumes
algebra, graphs, and elementary probability, not university mechanics or
calculus. Each symbol is introduced before use. Code in the source
appendix is actual implementation, not a fictional miniature presented
as a complete trainer.

The evidence snapshot concerns local experiments recorded on
\textbf{September 10, 2026}. The edition is \textbf{1.0, September 11,
2026}. A version number identifies a document snapshot; it does not mean
that every skill is solved.

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Question
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Evidence-backed answer
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Can a duck balance on a free basketball? & Yes, the fixed-command local
candidate survived all six recorded 60-second nominal trials. \\
Can it drive the ball reliably in the requested direction? & Not
established: zero of three forward-command trials passed the rolling
gate. \\
Does the suspended bridge exist as a working physical simulation? & Yes:
a free plank, four physical cable tendons, platforms, and visible
gantries. \\
Did the final bridge pilot complete an accepted crossing? & No.~It has
32,768 new PPO transitions and zero accepted crossings. \\
Are both examples available in Duck Viewer? & Yes. Seven cases means
five existing cases plus these two, not seven mastered skills. \\
Was either behavior verified on hardware? & No.~All results here are
simulation-only. \\
\end{longtable}
}

The successful basketball behavior began from a \textbf{supplied trained
recurrent actor}. The successful parts of this local experiment are
source import, accurate actor transfer, further PPO training, export
parity, sustained balance, and visible playback. This is not evidence
that a randomly initialized local network learned ball balance in
102,400 steps. Likewise, a successful bridge training command means the
pipeline ran; it does not mean the duck crossed.

``From scratch'' therefore has two meanings. You can reconstruct the
\textbf{software experiment} from source and required assets. You cannot
reconstruct an undocumented upstream training history or replace a
missing mature checkpoint with an assertion. This edition gives the
reproducible local path in full and marks the original policy as an
external prerequisite. The book's \texttt{source/} snapshot and ZIP are
supporting source, not a redistribution of all third-party robot meshes,
software dependencies, or policy weights.

\subsection{Choose a learning route}\label{choose-a-learning-route}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{First afternoon:} read the assumptions, inspect the scene
  diagrams, run preflight, run the pure-Python PPO arithmetic, and
  inspect real contact sheets. No training is necessary to learn the
  data flow.
\item
  \textbf{First experiment:} preserve the supplied artifacts, create a
  fresh run directory, execute a tiny smoke run, and inspect every
  output. A four-step smoke is an integration check only.
\item
  \textbf{Measured reproduction:} run the documented fixed-command
  basketball adaptation or the bridge pilot, export, evaluate
  deterministic policies with zero assistance, then inspect the complete
  rendered episode.
\item
  \textbf{Research extension:} change one physics or curriculum
  parameter, keep the evaluation fixed, run independent seeds, and
  document failures as carefully as successes.
\end{enumerate}

Read the PPO chapter before tuning a learning rate. Read the evaluation
chapter before deciding that a curve proves learning. Read the source
appendix alongside the case chapters rather than starting with thousands
of lines without context.

\subsection{A vocabulary we will keep
consistent}\label{a-vocabulary-we-will-keep-consistent}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Term
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Meaning in this book
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
State & All simulated physical variables, including information the
policy cannot see. \\
Observation & The 61 numbers provided to the actor at one control
step. \\
Action & Fourteen policy outputs interpreted by the robot control
contract. \\
Policy / actor & A function producing an action distribution during
training, and its mean during deterministic evaluation. \\
Critic / value & A learned estimate of future discounted reward, used
for training. \\
Episode & One attempt between reset and termination/time limit. \\
Transition & One observation--action--reward--next-observation
interaction in one environment. \\
Rollout & A block of transitions collected before updating the
network. \\
Checkpoint & Training weights plus whatever metadata that checkpoint
format actually stores. \\
ONNX export & Portable inference graph with the observation normalizer
baked in. \\
Curriculum & A sequence of easier-to-harder physics, spawning, or
judging conditions; not permission to change rewards silently. \\
Parity & Numerical agreement between two implementations on the same
inputs and recurrent-state lifecycle. \\
Skill gate & A measurable acceptance condition, distinct from process
exit or file creation. \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsection{Keep an experiment
notebook}\label{keep-an-experiment-notebook}

Record the source hashes, command, Python executable, package versions,
actuator mode, seed, spawn distribution, assistance level, randomization
settings, command distribution, step count, and evaluation horizon. Save
the full JSON result, not only a screenshot of its green region.

An example notebook entry is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{\{}
  \DataTypeTok{"experiment"}\FunctionTok{:} \StringTok{"my{-}independent{-}basketball{-}run"}\FunctionTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{"initialization"}\FunctionTok{:} \StringTok{"supplied actor; fresh critic and optimizer"}\FunctionTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{"control\_dt\_s"}\FunctionTok{:} \FloatTok{0.02}\FunctionTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{"actor\_observations"}\FunctionTok{:} \DecValTok{61}\FunctionTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{"actions"}\FunctionTok{:} \DecValTok{14}\FunctionTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{"claim\_to\_test"}\FunctionTok{:} \StringTok{"60{-}second free{-}ball balance"}\FunctionTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{"claim\_not\_yet\_supported"}\FunctionTok{:} \StringTok{"commanded rolling and steering"}\FunctionTok{,}
  \DataTypeTok{"hardware\_tested"}\FunctionTok{:} \KeywordTok{false}
\FunctionTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This is a template, not an additional measured run. The actual run
settings live beside the checkpoints. Filenames such as \texttt{final},
\texttt{full}, or \texttt{success} are not evidence by themselves.

\subsection{Where everything lives}\label{where-everything-lives}

All shell commands in this book start at the \textbf{workspace root},
unless a block explicitly changes directory. A path beginning
\texttt{rlx/} is relative to that root. Book assets and source snapshots
are relative to
\texttt{docs/\allowbreak{}basketba\allowbreak{}ll-\allowbreak{}bridge-\allowbreak{}book/\allowbreak{}}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{microduck{-}lab/}
\NormalTok{  microduck\_local/       CPU MuJoCo harness, robot contract, lab server}
\NormalTok{  microduck\_rl/          upstream robot/training reference checkout}
\NormalTok{  microduck/             upstream runtime and supplied walking policies}
\NormalTok{  microduck{-}playground/  supplied basketball experiment/assets/checkpoint}
\NormalTok{  rlx/                  local PPO, task environments, ONNX exports}
\NormalTok{  duck{-}viewer/           browser Studio and real scene playback}
\NormalTok{  scripts/              scenario command wrappers and API verification}
\NormalTok{  docs/basketball{-}bridge{-}book/}
\NormalTok{    chapters/en/        English chapter sources}
\NormalTok{    chapters/zh/        Chinese chapter sources}
\NormalTok{    assets/             measured plots, screenshots, schematic diagrams}
\NormalTok{    examples/           executable arithmetic and environment probes}
\NormalTok{    tools/              build, preflight, validation}
\NormalTok{    source/             exact selected implementation snapshot}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The full workspace is the execution unit. Copying one environment file
to an empty directory will not provide its robot contract, meshes,
actuator code, or training framework.

\section{Mathematical and physical
foundations}\label{mathematical-and-physical-foundations}

\subsection{Units before equations}\label{units-before-equations}

Use metres for length, seconds for time, kilograms for mass, radians for
angles, newtons for force, and newton-metres for torque. A value without
a unit can look reasonable while being wrong by a factor of a thousand.

At a 50 Hz control rate the actor is consulted 50 times each second:

\[\Delta t = 1/50 = 0.02\ \mathrm{s}.\]

A 60-second basketball test contains 3,000 control steps. A 20-second
bridge episode contains 1,000. These are control steps; the physics
engine can perform multiple smaller integration steps inside each one.
More environments increase data per rollout, not the duration of one
simulated second.

Velocity is change of position divided by elapsed time. If the duck
moves 0.03 m in 0.2 s, its average speed is 0.15 m/s. Acceleration is
change of velocity divided by elapsed time. These finite differences are
enough to understand the simulator's job before studying continuous
derivatives.

Forces change momentum. In one dimension, a simple teaching update is

\[v_{t+1}=v_t+(F/m)\Delta t,\qquad x_{t+1}=x_t+v_{t+1}\Delta t.\]

This is an explanatory approximation, \textbf{not} a replacement for
MuJoCo's integration, articulated-body dynamics, and contact solver. The
actual simulator also resolves joints, contacts, friction and
constraints. A torque is a turning effect: force times its perpendicular
lever arm. A foot force displaced from the plank's centre can rotate it
even when the duck remains upright.

\subsection{Why balance and motion
interact}\label{why-balance-and-motion-interact}

On a floor, the support under a planted foot is nearly fixed. On the
ball, a foot tangential force can rotate the ball and move the support.
If an ideal rigid sphere rolls without slip, distance and angle satisfy

\[d=r\theta.\]

With radius 0.12 m, a 90-degree rotation is \(\theta=\pi/2\) radians,
giving \(d\approx0.1885\) m. At 0.15 m/s the ideal angular speed is
\(v/r=1.25\) rad/s. These are scale checks, not guarantees: slipping,
contact deformation and turning break the simple straight-line relation.

For the suspended bridge, gravity pulls the combined masses down, while
cables support and constrain the plank. A cable is not a rigid beam: its
tension acts along its length. The implementation's tendon and limit
settings approximate the desired cable behavior; their exact meaning is
determined by the model, not by how the rendered cable looks. A gantry
can be visible while having no collision role. The visual scene is
evidence of appearance, not a complete description of the dynamics.

A material name such as ``wood'' or ``rubber'' does not automatically
create correct physics. The simulator needs mass, dimensions, inertia,
friction/contact parameters, joint and tendon definitions. The wooden
appearance is a visual material. The 0.45 kg plank mass is a dynamic
parameter. They must be documented separately.

\subsection{Coordinate frames: forward for
whom?}\label{coordinate-frames-forward-for-whom}

The \textbf{world frame} belongs to the scene. The \textbf{body frame}
turns with the duck. If the duck rotates 90 degrees, body-forward no
longer points along world-x. Rewarding world-x velocity while issuing
body-forward commands creates a misleading task.

For a planar heading angle \(\psi\), a body-frame velocity can be
expressed in world coordinates as

\[v_x^w=\cos\psi\,v_x^b-\sin\psi\,v_y^b,\]
\[v_y^w=\sin\psi\,v_x^b+\cos\psi\,v_y^b.\]

Superscripts \(w\) and \(b\) mean world and body, not exponentiation.
With body velocity \((0.15,0)\) and heading 90 degrees, world velocity
is approximately \((0,0.15)\). The basketball evaluator uses an
initial-heading reference for directional progress; the bridge commander
translates navigation information into ordinary body-frame commands.
Neither fact makes world position an actor input.

\subsection{Feedback, memory, and partial
observation}\label{feedback-memory-and-partial-observation}

An \textbf{open-loop} script repeats preset joint targets regardless of
what happens. A \textbf{feedback policy} chooses the next targets using
current sensed information. If a foot slips, the next observation
changes and the policy can respond.

The robot does not receive the entire simulator state. It gets angular
velocity, projected gravity, commands, joint information, previous
actions and reserved command slots. It does not directly receive ball
centre or plank pose. This is partial observation. Two identical current
observations may have different motion histories. The basketball actor
therefore carries LSTM memory; the bridge actor is a transferred
feedforward walking actor. A memory network is not clairvoyance: it can
only summarize evidence that entered its observations.

During learning, random sampled actions help explore nearby behavior.
During deterministic evaluation, the exported actor's mean is used. A
stochastic policy that occasionally catches itself may export to a mean
policy that falls. That is why training reward and exported-policy
testing are different experiments.

\subsection{Arithmetic workshop}\label{arithmetic-workshop}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ math}

\NormalTok{control\_dt }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.02}
\NormalTok{radius }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.12}
\NormalTok{command\_speed }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.15}
\NormalTok{steps }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{60} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ control\_dt)}
\NormalTok{quarter\_turn\_distance }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ radius }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ math.pi }\OperatorTok{/} \DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{ideal\_angular\_speed }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ command\_speed }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ radius}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(steps, }\BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(quarter\_turn\_distance, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), ideal\_angular\_speed)}
\ControlFlowTok{assert}\NormalTok{ steps }\OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{3000}
\ControlFlowTok{assert} \BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(ideal\_angular\_speed }\OperatorTok{{-}} \FloatTok{1.25}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{1e{-}12}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Expected output: \texttt{3000\ 0.1885\ 1.25}. This is a unit test of
arithmetic, not a balance test.

\textbf{Exercise.} Four environments collect 128 steps before each
update. How many transitions are collected? How much simulated time
elapses in each lane? How many such updates make 32,768 transitions?

\textbf{Answer.} \(4\times128=512\) transitions; each lane advances
\(128\times0.02=2.56\) seconds; \(32768/512=64\) updates. Summing lane
durations gives aggregate simulated experience, not one continuous
655.36-second bridge crossing.

\textbf{Exercise.} Why can a high average reward fail to prove a
60-second hold?

\textbf{Answer.} Short successful segments separated by falls/resets can
have high average reward. A sustained hold requires one unbroken
episode, first-fall accounting, valid foot support, and the entire
requested duration.

\section{Set up a reproducible
workshop}\label{set-up-a-reproducible-workshop}

\subsection{Materials: simulation assets are not a hardware shopping
list}\label{materials-simulation-assets-are-not-a-hardware-shopping-list}

The following is the \textbf{simulation bill of materials}. It
identifies objects and files you need to reproduce the software
experiment. It is not a certified mechanical design, purchasing
recommendation, or construction specification for putting an expensive
robot above a moving plank.

Suspended plank & One & Length 1.10 m, width 0.13 m, thickness 0.03 m,
mass 0.45 kg. \\
Cable tendons & Four & Physical constraints/support, not only decorative
lines. \\
Launch and destination platforms & Two & Static support and unambiguous
start/end regions. \\
Visible gantries & Scene components & Show anchor placement;
non-colliding visual structure. \\
Ground plane, lighting, camera & One scene setup & Ground contact is
detected as failure, not hidden by the camera. \\
Supplied basketball actor and reference ONNX & One pair & Required
mature initializer for the measured local experiment. \\
Supplied walking ONNX & One & Exact bridge actor/normalizer
initializer. \\
\end{longtable}
}

No physical timber grade, cable breaking load, fastening specification,
battery limit, servo thermal envelope or safety factor was measured for
this book. Do not infer those from simulated dimensions. Real tests
would need engineered restraints, an exclusion zone, an emergency stop,
torque/current limits, qualified supervision, and separate sim-to-real
validation. The local harness is a prototype, not deployment approval.

\subsection{Computer and software
assumptions}\label{computer-and-software-assumptions}

The recorded path uses an Apple-Silicon Mac. MuJoCo steps the physical
scene on CPU; basketball PPO uses PyTorch CPU; bridge PPO uses
MLX/Metal. ``No CUDA required'' does \textbf{not} mean ``every part runs
without a GPU runtime on every operating system.'' This edition does not
claim a clean Linux reproduction of the bridge learner.

Use Python 3.12, not whichever \texttt{python} happens to be first in
your shell. Node/npm run the viewer. \texttt{uv} manages Python
environments. Pandoc, XeLaTeX, \texttt{pdfinfo}, \texttt{pdftotext}, and
\texttt{pdftoppm} build and inspect the book; they are not needed for
training. Pillow and Matplotlib generate plots, not policy actions.

The measured Python package versions are recorded automatically in
\texttt{environm\allowbreak{}ent.json\allowbreak{}}. This is a
\textbf{recorded environment inventory}, not a tested universal resolver
lock. Source changes outside a Git commit are bound by file SHA-256
values in \texttt{source-\allowbreak{}manifest\allowbreak{}.json}; a
commit hash alone would miss the new uncommitted skill implementations.

\subsection{Acquire the workspace, not just the
PDF}\label{acquire-the-workspace-not-just-the-pdf}

Obtain this workspace checkout from its maintainer, including its
\texttt{rlx} checkout, upstream sibling repositories and the
user-supplied playground references. Do not invent a public download URL
for policy weights. Before installing anything, inspect the local
prerequisites:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{test} \AttributeTok{{-}f}\NormalTok{ microduck\_local/src/microduck\_local/contract.py}
\BuiltInTok{test} \AttributeTok{{-}f}\NormalTok{ rlx/rlx/mjlab\_microduck/robot/microduck/robot\_allcollisions.xml}
\BuiltInTok{test} \AttributeTok{{-}f}\NormalTok{ microduck/policies/alpha\_walking.onnx}
\BuiltInTok{test} \AttributeTok{{-}f}\NormalTok{ microduck{-}playground/artifacts/basketball/checkpoint.pt}
\BuiltInTok{test} \AttributeTok{{-}f}\NormalTok{ microduck{-}playground/artifacts/basketball/policy.onnx}
\BuiltInTok{test} \AttributeTok{{-}f}\NormalTok{ microduck{-}playground/src/mjlab\_microduck/robot/assets/basketball/basketball.obj}
\BuiltInTok{test} \AttributeTok{{-}f}\NormalTok{ microduck{-}playground/src/mjlab\_microduck/robot/assets/basketball/basketball.png}
\BuiltInTok{command} \AttributeTok{{-}v}\NormalTok{ uv node npm}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If a test fails, stop at that missing asset. Changing the actor
architecture or replacing its normalizer with zeros does not repair a
missing checkpoint. The bridge scene itself uses the packaged robot
assets; basketball additionally requires the supplied playground
OBJ/PNG. Walking baselines and other harness paths may require the
upstream sibling model checkout.

For a new environment, the repo's documented setup route is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ rlx}
\ExtensionTok{uv}\NormalTok{ sync }\AttributeTok{{-}{-}python}\NormalTok{ 3.12}
\ExtensionTok{uv}\NormalTok{ pip install }\AttributeTok{{-}{-}python}\NormalTok{ .venv/bin/python }\AttributeTok{{-}e}\NormalTok{ ../microduck\_local}
\ExtensionTok{.venv/bin/python}\NormalTok{ examples/ppo\_microduck\_studio.py }\AttributeTok{{-}{-}help}
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ ..}
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ duck{-}viewer}
\ExtensionTok{npm}\NormalTok{ ci}
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ ..}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

These installation commands require network/package availability. The
book's validation uses the existing recorded environment and does not
claim to have recreated a fresh machine offline. Preserve lockfiles;
compare package versions and test before changing a resolved dependency.
Do not copy a virtual environment between computers.

The recorded executable is
\texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}.venv-\allowbreak{}microduc\allowbreak{}k/\allowbreak{}bin/\allowbreak{}python}.
The clean setup above creates
\texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}.venv/\allowbreak{}bin/\allowbreak{}python}.
Substitute the latter consistently in the case chapters if that is your
environment. The following variable makes the distinction explicit:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\VariableTok{PYTHON}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"}\VariableTok{$PWD}\StringTok{/rlx/.venv{-}microduck/bin/python"}
\BuiltInTok{test} \AttributeTok{{-}x} \StringTok{"}\VariableTok{$PYTHON}\StringTok{"}
\StringTok{"}\VariableTok{$PYTHON}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{ docs/basketball{-}bridge{-}book/tools/preflight.py}
\StringTok{"}\VariableTok{$PYTHON}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{ docs/basketball{-}bridge{-}book/examples/ppo\_arithmetic.py}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Preflight checks required local assets and reports package versions
without importing Metal. It does not prove that a subsequent Metal or
graphics context can be opened. A restricted sandbox may permit file
reads but reject Metal or CoreGraphics. That is a runtime permission
problem, not proof of a broken actor.

evaluation}\label{separate-preflight-smoke-training-and-evaluation}

These tests exercise the named implementation boundary. They do not

\subsection{Before the first step: inspect the
scene}\label{before-the-first-step-inspect-the-scene}

Run the book's environment probe to compile both models, check the
observation/action shape and sample four finite transitions. It applies
zero action and records a construction check; it does not load a learned
policy or prove balance:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{rlx/.venv{-}microduck/bin/python} \DataTypeTok{\textbackslash{}}
\NormalTok{  docs/basketball{-}bridge{-}book/examples/probe\_scenes.py}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Each environment owns mutable MuJoCo data. Shared immutable model
resources can save memory, but recurrent hidden states and per-duck
simulation states must never be shared. A viewer can display two ducks
using the same ONNX session while each maintains independent LSTM
memory.

\subsection{Preserve outputs before a new
run}\label{preserve-outputs-before-a-new-run}

Use new output directories, such as
\texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}runs/\allowbreak{}book/\allowbreak{}basketba\allowbreak{}ll-\allowbreak{}YYYYMMDD\allowbreak{}-\allowbreak{}HHMMSS},
replacing the date token with a fresh run identifier. Check the
destination does not already exist. Never train into the evidence
directories printed in this book.

For each run, preserve at least:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the executed command, software inventory, source hash and
  source-policy hash;
\item
  checkpoint and associated metadata, final ONNX and parity result;
\item
  full update/episode logs, not only a smoothed reward graph;
\item
  evaluation JSON including failures, seeds, horizon and assistance
  settings;
\item
  video, contact sheet and render metadata bound to the evaluated
  policy.
\end{itemize}

A checkpoint is not automatically a complete optimizer resume. In these
two pathways, source actors are transferred but critics/optimizers are
new. Re-running a command may reproduce the protocol without reproducing
identical floating-point weights. Different numerical libraries, package
versions, scheduling and simulator versions can change trajectories. The
honest goal is a traceable experiment and matching acceptance criteria,
not an unjustified promise of bitwise retraining.

\subsection{Troubleshooting without changing the
question}\label{troubleshooting-without-changing-the-question}

\textbf{Exercise.} Why preserve both policy hashes and video evidence?
\textbf{Answer:} a good-looking video can come from a different policy.
Hash-bound metadata connects the claim, checkpoint, deterministic
export, evaluation and visible trajectory.

\section{PPO: Learning Carefully from a Short Batch of
Experience}\label{ppo-learning-carefully-from-a-short-batch-of-experience}

Proximal Policy Optimization, usually shortened to \textbf{PPO}, is the
learning algorithm used by the local RLX trainer. PPO does not directly
teach a robot what basketball means. The environment supplies
observations, actions, rewards, and episode boundaries. PPO uses those
records to answer a narrower question:

\begin{quote}
Given the actions the current policy just tried, which actions should
become a little more likely, which should become a little less likely,
and how large a change is safe enough to attempt?
\end{quote}

This chapter develops that answer from high-school algebra, probability
density, and averages. It then maps each idea to the repository's
current implementation:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}examples\allowbreak{}/\allowbreak{}ppo\_\allowbreak{}microduc\allowbreak{}k\_\allowbreak{}basketba\allowbreak{}ll.py}:
  the recurrent PyTorch continuation trainer for the released basketball
  actor;
\item
  \texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}rlx/\allowbreak{}algorith\allowbreak{}ms/\allowbreak{}ppo.py}:
  the generic feedforward MLX PPO implementation;
\item
  \texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}rlx/\allowbreak{}buffers/\allowbreak{}rollout\_\allowbreak{}buffer.p\allowbreak{}y}:
  the fixed-size rollout storage used by the MLX implementation.
\end{itemize}

The code is the authority for implementation details. PPO papers explain
the general method, but this repository adds choices such as
timeout-aware bootstrapping, a robust critic loss, numerical log-ratio
clipping, and a basketball-specific KL stopping rule.

\subsection{The learning loop in plain
language}\label{the-learning-loop-in-plain-language}

Imagine coaching a player who must act every 20 milliseconds. At each
tick:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The policy receives an observation, such as joint state, orientation,
  command, and recent motion information.
\item
  The actor produces a probability distribution over 14 continuous joint
  commands.
\item
  One action is sampled from that distribution.
\item
  The simulator advances and returns a reward plus boundary flags.
\item
  The critic predicts how much discounted future reward was expected
  from the old observation.
\end{enumerate}

PPO saves a short block of these transitions. It then replays the saved
actions through the updated policy and compares:

\[
\text{old log probability}
\quad\text{with}\quad
\text{new log probability}.
\]

If an action turned out better than the critic expected, PPO tries to
increase its probability. If it turned out worse, PPO tries to decrease
its probability. The word \textbf{proximal} means that the update is
deliberately conservative: PPO limits how much benefit it can claim from
moving the probability ratio too far in the helpful direction.

After several optimizer passes, the rollout is discarded. New data must
be collected from the new policy. PPO is therefore \textbf{on-policy}:
it does not keep an unlimited replay database containing actions from
many old policies.

The two implementations in this repository organize that same idea
differently:

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Path
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Actor memory
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Update organization
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Main numerical framework
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Generic
\texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}rlx/\allowbreak{}algorith\allowbreak{}ms/\allowbreak{}ppo.py}
& Feedforward & Flatten time and environments, shuffle, use minibatches
& MLX \\
Basketball
\texttt{ppo\_\allowbreak{}microduc\allowbreak{}k\_\allowbreak{}basketba\allowbreak{}ll.py}
& One-layer LSTM & Replay the entire time-by-environment sequence each
epoch & PyTorch \\
\end{longtable}
}

That difference is not cosmetic. A feedforward sample can be trained as
an independent row. An LSTM action depends on the hidden state produced
by earlier observations, so its history must be reconstructed in the
correct order.

\subsection{Actor, critic, and the meaning of
advantage}\label{actor-critic-and-the-meaning-of-advantage}

PPO trains two prediction systems.

The \textbf{actor} chooses actions. For continuous control, it produces
a mean action \(\mu(o)\) and a standard deviation \(\sigma\). The
basketball actor has 14 action dimensions. Its recurrent core first
processes a 61-dimensional observation with an LSTM of hidden size 256,
then an MLP maps the recurrent output through widths 512, 256, and 128
to 14 means.

The \textbf{critic} predicts a scalar value:

\[
V(o_t)\approx
\mathbb E[r_t+\gamma r_{t+1}+\gamma^2r_{t+2}+\cdots].
\]

The discount \(\gamma\) makes distant rewards count less. With
\(\gamma=0.99\), a reward one step away is multiplied by \(0.99\), two
steps away by \(0.99^2\), and so on.

The actor does not train directly from raw reward. It trains from an
\textbf{advantage estimate}:

\[
\widehat A_t \approx
\text{outcome after action }a_t
-
\text{critic's expectation before }a_t.
\]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\widehat A_t>0\): the sampled action did better than the baseline;
  make it more likely.
\item
  \(\widehat A_t<0\): it did worse than the baseline; make it less
  likely.
\item
  \(\widehat A_t\approx0\): the sample provides little evidence for
  changing its probability.
\end{itemize}

``Positive advantage'' does not mean ``positive reward.'' A reward of 2
can have negative advantage if the critic expected 5. A reward of \(-1\)
can have positive advantage if the critic expected \(-4\). Advantage is
a comparison with a baseline.

The basketball critic is feedforward even though the actor is recurrent.
It reads the current normalized 61-vector and predicts one value. The
actor and critic share the actor's frozen observation normalizer, but
the basketball continuation creates a \textbf{fresh critic and fresh
Adam optimizer}. It is an actor warm start, not a full
optimizer-and-critic resume.

\subsection{Continuous actions use probability
density}\label{continuous-actions-use-probability-density}

For one action coordinate, a Gaussian policy has density

\[
p(a\mid\mu,\sigma)
=
\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\left(
-\frac{(a-\mu)^2}{2\sigma^2}
\right).
\]

The corresponding log density is

\[
\log p(a\mid\mu,\sigma)
=
-\frac12\left[
\frac{(a-\mu)^2}{\sigma^2}
+2\log\sigma
+\log(2\pi)
\right].
\]

For 14 independent Gaussian coordinates, the implementation sums 14 log
densities:

\[
\log\pi(a\mid o)
=
\sum_{j=1}^{14}\log p(a_j\mid\mu_j(o),\sigma_j).
\]

This produces one joint log probability per transition.

\subsubsection{Density can exceed 1}\label{density-can-exceed-1}

Students often learn that a probability cannot exceed 1. That statement
is true for the probability of an event, but a continuous Gaussian
formula returns a \textbf{density}, not the probability of one exact
point. A narrow density can be taller than 1 as long as the total area
under the curve remains 1.

For \(\mu=0\) and \(\sigma=0.1\), the density at the mean is

\[
p(0)=\frac{1}{0.1\sqrt{2\pi}}\approx3.989.
\]

The probability of landing in an interval is the area over that
interval. For a tiny interval of width \(0.01\) near the mean, the
probability is roughly density times width, about
\(3.989\times0.01=0.03989\), still below 1.

The log density can therefore be positive. That is legal. A positive
continuous log density does not mean a probability greater than 1.

The following complete standard-library program reproduces the
calculation:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ math}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ gaussian\_log\_density(x: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{, mean: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{, std: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{:}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ std }\OperatorTok{\textless{}=} \FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"std must be positive"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    variance }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ std }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ std}
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.5} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}
\NormalTok{        (x }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{**} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ variance}
        \OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{2.0} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ math.log(std)}
        \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ math.log(}\FloatTok{2.0} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ math.pi)}
\NormalTok{    )}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{    log\_density }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ gaussian\_log\_density(}\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    density }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ math.exp(log\_density)}
\NormalTok{    approximate\_interval\_probability }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ density }\OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{0.01}

    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"log density: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{log\_density}\SpecialCharTok{:.6f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"density: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{density}\SpecialCharTok{:.6f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"approximate probability in width 0.01: "}
          \SpecialStringTok{f"}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{approximate\_interval\_probability}\SpecialCharTok{:.6f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

    \ControlFlowTok{assert}\NormalTok{ density }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{1.0}
    \ControlFlowTok{assert} \FloatTok{0.0} \OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ approximate\_interval\_probability }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{1.0}


\ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{\_\_name\_\_} \OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{"\_\_main\_\_"}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{    main()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Expected output, up to final-digit rounding:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{log density: 1.383647}
\NormalTok{density: 3.989423}
\NormalTok{approximate probability in width 0.01: 0.039894}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

In
\texttt{ppo\_\allowbreak{}microduc\allowbreak{}k\_\allowbreak{}basketba\allowbreak{}ll.py},
\texttt{gaussian\_log\_prob()} implements the same expression with Torch
tensors and sums over the action dimension. \texttt{gaussian\_entropy()}
also sums over dimensions.

\subsection{From log probabilities to the PPO
ratio}\label{from-log-probabilities-to-the-ppo-ratio}

The stored action was sampled under the old policy. PPO asks how its
density changes under the new policy:

\[
r_t(\theta)
=
\frac{\pi_\theta(a_t\mid o_t)}
{\pi_{\theta_{\mathrm{old}}}(a_t\mid o_t)}.
\]

Direct division of tiny densities is numerically awkward, so code uses
log probabilities:

\[
r_t(\theta)
=
\exp\left(
\log\pi_\theta(a_t\mid o_t)
-
\log\pi_{\theta_{\mathrm{old}}}(a_t\mid o_t)
\right).
\]

The ratio is always positive because an exponential is positive.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(r=1\): the saved action has the same density.
\item
  \(r=1.2\): its density is 20\% larger.
\item
  \(r=0.7\): its density is 30\% smaller.
\end{itemize}

The ratio is not ``the probability of the action.'' It is a comparison
of new and old densities for the same recorded action.

The generic MLX code clips the \textbf{log ratio} to
\texttt{{[}-20,\ 20{]}} before exponentiating. This is a numerical
overflow guard. It is separate from PPO's much narrower policy clip such
as \([0.8,1.2]\). The basketball trainer currently exponentiates the
unbounded log-ratio directly, then rejects non-finite KL values or
losses.

\subsection{The clipped policy
objective}\label{the-clipped-policy-objective}

PPO's sample-level surrogate is

\[
L_t^{\mathrm{clip}}
=
\min\left(
r_t\widehat A_t,\;
\operatorname{clip}(r_t,1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)\widehat A_t
\right).
\]

The algorithm wants to maximize this quantity. The implementation
minimizes its negative. The generic MLX source writes the negative form
as a \texttt{maximum}; the basketball source writes the positive form
with \texttt{minimum} and negates the mean. They are algebraically
equivalent.

With \(\epsilon=0.2\), the clipped interval is \([0.8,1.2]\).

\subsubsection{Positive advantage}\label{positive-advantage}

Suppose \(\widehat A=+2\). Increasing the ratio is helpful because it
makes a good action more likely.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  At \(r=1.1\), both terms equal \(2.2\).
\item
  At \(r=1.3\), the ordinary product is \(2.6\), but the clipped product
  is \(1.2\times2=2.4\). PPO uses \(2.4\).
\item
  At \(r=0.7\), the ordinary product is \(1.4\) and the clipped product
  is \(1.6\). PPO uses the smaller value, \(1.4\).
\end{itemize}

The objective stops rewarding an overly large helpful increase, but it
does not erase a harmful decrease.

\subsubsection{Negative advantage}\label{negative-advantage}

Suppose \(\widehat A=-2\). Decreasing the ratio is helpful because it
makes a bad action less likely.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  At \(r=0.7\), the ordinary product is \(-1.4\), while the clipped
  product is \(0.8\times(-2)=-1.6\). PPO uses the smaller value,
  \(-1.6\).
\item
  At \(r=1.3\), the ordinary product is \(-2.6\) and the clipped product
  is \(-2.4\). PPO uses \(-2.6\).
\end{itemize}

For negative advantage, the helpful direction is downward. The plateau
appears below \(0.8\), not above \(1.2\).

This standalone program prints both cases:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ clipped\_surrogate(ratio: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{, advantage: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{, epsilon: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{    clipped\_ratio }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{min}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(ratio, }\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ epsilon), }\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ epsilon)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \BuiltInTok{min}\NormalTok{(ratio }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ advantage, clipped\_ratio }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ advantage)}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ advantage }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{2.0}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{2.0}\NormalTok{):}
        \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"advantage = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{advantage}\SpecialCharTok{:+.1f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ ratio }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{0.7}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.3}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{            value }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ clipped\_surrogate(ratio, advantage)}
            \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"  ratio=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{ratio}\SpecialCharTok{:.1f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, surrogate=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{value}\SpecialCharTok{:+.1f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

    \ControlFlowTok{assert}\NormalTok{ clipped\_surrogate(}\FloatTok{1.3}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.0}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{==} \FloatTok{2.4}
    \ControlFlowTok{assert}\NormalTok{ clipped\_surrogate(}\FloatTok{0.7}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{2.0}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{==} \OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{1.6}


\ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{\_\_name\_\_} \OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{"\_\_main\_\_"}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{    main()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Clipping is not a guarantee that the new policy is better. It only
changes the training objective for the sampled batch. The network can
still change on observations absent from that batch, multiple parameters
interact, the critic can be wrong, and the reward can fail to represent
the intended basketball skill.

\begin{figure}
\centering
\includegraphics[width=6.2in,height=5.5in,alt={Schematic PPO clipping curves. This explanatory diagram is not measured training evidence.}]{assets/diagrams/schematic-ppo-clipping.png}
\caption{Schematic PPO clipping curves. This explanatory diagram is not
measured training evidence.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Generalized Advantage
Estimation}\label{generalized-advantage-estimation}

The one-step temporal-difference residual is

\[
\delta_t
=
r_t+\gamma b_t-V(o_t),
\]

where \(b_t\) is the permitted bootstrap value after the transition.
Generalized Advantage Estimation, or GAE, combines residuals backward:

\[
\widehat A_t
=
\delta_t+\gamma\lambda m_t\widehat A_{t+1}.
\]

Here:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\gamma\) discounts future return;
\item
  \(\lambda\) controls how strongly later residuals affect the current
  estimate;
\item
  \(m_t\) is a continuation mask that prevents a trace from crossing an
  episode reset.
\end{itemize}

With the repository defaults \(\gamma=0.99\) and \(\lambda=0.95\), the
trace multiplier is

\[
\gamma\lambda=0.9405.
\]

A smaller \(\lambda\) relies more on the critic's one-step predictions.
A larger \(\lambda\) carries more sampled future information backward,
usually with more sampling variance. Neither value is universally best.

The critic target is

\[
\widehat R_t=\widehat A_t+V(o_t).
\]

The actor uses \(\widehat A_t\); the critic fits \(\widehat R_t\).

\subsection{Terminal and time-limit masks are
different}\label{terminal-and-time-limit-masks-are-different}

Gymnasium separates two boundaries:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{termination}: the modeled task truly ended, such as a failure
  condition;
\item
  \textbf{truncation}: collection stopped for an external reason,
  commonly a time limit, although the underlying task could continue.
\end{itemize}

Both boundaries reset the environment. They do not receive the same
value bootstrap.

For transition \(t\), define:

\[
b_t=
\begin{cases}
0,&\text{if terminated},\\
V(o_{t+1}^{\mathrm{final}}),&\text{if truncated but not terminated},\\
V(o_{t+1}),&\text{if the episode continues}.
\end{cases}
\]

The GAE trace mask is zero for either termination or truncation:

\[
m_t=1-\mathbf 1[\text{terminated}\lor\text{truncated}].
\]

This gives two separate decisions:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{May the current residual bootstrap a value?} A pure timeout
  may; a true terminal may not.
\item
  \textbf{May advantage flow into the next stored transition?} Neither
  boundary may, because the next stored observation can belong to a
  reset episode.
\end{enumerate}

The basketball implementation stores
\texttt{bootstra\allowbreak{}p\_\allowbreak{}values} for every
transition. During collection, it evaluates the critic on the final
pre-reset observation, then explicitly replaces the value with zero
where \texttt{terminated} is true. Its \texttt{compute\_gae()} uses:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{\textasciitilde{}terminated} as the bootstrap mask;
\item
  \texttt{\textasciitilde{}(terminated\ \textbar{}\ truncated)} as the
  trace-continuation mask.
\end{itemize}

The generic MLX implementation reaches the same semantics differently.
\texttt{\_truncation\_values()} selects only
\texttt{truncated\ \&\ \textasciitilde{}terminated} environments and
evaluates \texttt{info{[}"terminal\_observation"{]}}. The shared GAE
helper uses the timeout value for a truncation, zero for termination,
and the ordinary next value otherwise. It never bootstraps from an
autoreset observation.

\subsubsection{Worked boundary example}\label{worked-boundary-example}

Suppose:

\[
r_t=1,\quad V(o_t)=2,\quad
V(o_{t+1}^{\mathrm{final}})=5,\quad \gamma=0.99.
\]

If the transition is a true terminal:

\[
\delta_t=1+0-2=-1.
\]

If it is only a time limit:

\[
\delta_t=1+0.99(5)-2=3.95.
\]

If an autoreset observation has value 99, using it would give

\[
1+0.99(99)-2=97.01,
\]

which incorrectly assigns value from the next episode to the previous
one.

The next program computes a three-step trace and proves that a reward in
the reset episode cannot leak backward:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Sequence}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ gae(}
\NormalTok{    rewards: Sequence[}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{    values: Sequence[}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{    bootstraps: Sequence[}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{    terminated: Sequence[}\BuiltInTok{bool}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{    truncated: Sequence[}\BuiltInTok{bool}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{    gamma: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.99}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    gae\_lambda: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.95}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{tuple}\NormalTok{[}\BuiltInTok{list}\NormalTok{[}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{], }\BuiltInTok{list}\NormalTok{[}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{]]:}
\NormalTok{    size }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(rewards)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not} \BuiltInTok{all}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(x) }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ size }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ (}
\NormalTok{        values, bootstraps, terminated, truncated}
\NormalTok{    )):}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"all inputs must have equal length"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{    advantages }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ size}
\NormalTok{    trace }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ step }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(size }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{        bootstrap\_mask }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ terminated[step] }\ControlFlowTok{else} \FloatTok{1.0}
\NormalTok{        continuation\_mask }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}
            \FloatTok{0.0} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ terminated[step] }\KeywordTok{or}\NormalTok{ truncated[step] }\ControlFlowTok{else} \FloatTok{1.0}
\NormalTok{        )}
\NormalTok{        delta }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}
\NormalTok{            rewards[step]}
            \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ gamma }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ bootstraps[step] }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ bootstrap\_mask}
            \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ values[step]}
\NormalTok{        )}
\NormalTok{        trace }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ delta }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ gamma }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ gae\_lambda }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ continuation\_mask }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ trace}
\NormalTok{        advantages[step] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ trace}

\NormalTok{    returns }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [a }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ v }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ a, v }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{zip}\NormalTok{(advantages, values)]}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ advantages, returns}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{    advantages, returns }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ gae(}
\NormalTok{        rewards}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{        values}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.2}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.1}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{        bootstraps}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\FloatTok{1.2}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.9}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{        terminated}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{, }\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{, }\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{        truncated}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{, }\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{, }\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{    )}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{([}\BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ advantages])}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{([}\BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ returns])}

\NormalTok{    changed\_next\_episode\_reward }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1000.0}
    \ControlFlowTok{assert}\NormalTok{ changed\_next\_episode\_reward }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.0}  \CommentTok{\# It is deliberately unused.}
    \ControlFlowTok{assert} \BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(advantages[}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{==} \OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.209}
    \ControlFlowTok{assert}\NormalTok{ [}\BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ advantages] }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ [}
        \FloatTok{0.586836}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.107565}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.209}
\NormalTok{    ]}


\ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{\_\_name\_\_} \OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{"\_\_main\_\_"}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{    main()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The expected advantages are:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{[0.586836, {-}0.107565, {-}0.209]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The timeout transition bootstraps from 0.9, but its continuation mask is
zero, so no later reset-episode advantage enters the trace.

\subsection{Advantage normalization}\label{advantage-normalization}

Raw advantages can have very different scales across tasks or batches.
Both implementations normalize advantages:

\[
\widehat A'_t
=
\frac{\widehat A_t-\operatorname{mean}(\widehat A)}
{\operatorname{std}(\widehat A)+10^{-8}}.
\]

The basketball trainer normalizes once over the complete rollout before
its epoch loop. The generic MLX trainer normalizes inside
\texttt{\_loss\_and\_metrics()} after selecting a minibatch, so each
actual minibatch uses its own mean and standard deviation.

Normalization changes scale and can change a sample's sign relative to
the selected group's mean. It does not change rewards, returns, or old
log probabilities. It also does not mean that an advantage of
\texttt{+2} in a teaching table will literally remain \texttt{+2} in
training.

\subsection{The critic loss}\label{the-critic-loss}

The basketball continuation uses ordinary half squared error:

\[
L_V
=
\frac12\operatorname{mean}\left[
(V_{\mathrm{new}}-\widehat R)^2
\right].
\]

It does not clip the value update.

The generic MLX PPO uses a Huber-style error with threshold 10:

\[
h(e)=
\begin{cases}
\frac12e^2,&|e|\le10,\\
10|e|-50,&|e|>10.
\end{cases}
\]

When \texttt{clip\_value\_loss=True}, it also creates a clipped
prediction:

\[
V_{\mathrm{clip}}
=
V_{\mathrm{old}}+
\operatorname{clip}(V_{\mathrm{new}}-V_{\mathrm{old}},-\epsilon,\epsilon),
\]

then uses the larger of the unclipped and clipped Huber errors. Choosing
the larger prevents the clipped branch from making an excessively
changed value prediction look artificially good.

This is an important implementation difference. ``RLX PPO uses value
clipping'' is true for
\texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}rlx/\allowbreak{}algorith\allowbreak{}ms/\allowbreak{}ppo.py},
but false for the basketball-specific \texttt{ppo\_update()}.

\subsection{Entropy and why it can be
negative}\label{entropy-and-why-it-can-be-negative}

Entropy encourages exploration. For one Gaussian coordinate,

\[
H
=
\log\sigma+\frac12\log(2\pi e).
\]

For independent coordinates, the implementation sums this value.

This is \textbf{differential entropy}, not discrete Shannon entropy.
Differential entropy may be negative. For example, with \(\sigma=0.03\):

\[
H_{\text{one dimension}}\approx-2.087,
\]

so 14 equal dimensions have total entropy near \(-29.2\).

Nothing is wrong with a negative value. It means the continuous density
is concentrated in a narrow region. It does not mean negative
probability.

Both loss functions subtract the entropy bonus:

\[
L_{\mathrm{total}}
=
L_{\mathrm{policy}}
+c_VL_V
-c_HH.
\]

If \(H\) is negative, the term \(-c_HH\) is positive. The optimizer
still follows the exact differential-entropy objective; the sign should
not be interpreted with discrete-entropy intuition.

The basketball actor stores a directly trained standard-deviation
parameter and clamps it only to a minimum of \texttt{1e-6} when used.
Loading a source actor then fills this parameter with the requested
\texttt{initial\_std}, whose command-line default is \texttt{0.03}. Thus
the continuation deliberately resets exploration scale instead of
retaining the source checkpoint's saved standard deviation.

\subsection{Total loss is not reward, and neither proves
mastery}\label{total-loss-is-not-reward-and-neither-proves-mastery}

The basketball total loss is

\[
L
=
L_{\mathrm{policy}}
+1.0L_V
-0.01H
\quad\text{under its defaults}.
\]

The generic PPO defaults use value coefficient \texttt{0.5} and entropy
coefficient \texttt{0.01}, although example programs can override them.

A falling training loss is not the same as a rising episode reward:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  policy loss measures a clipped probability objective on the latest
  batch;
\item
  value loss measures critic prediction error;
\item
  entropy measures distribution spread;
\item
  reward comes from the environment;
\item
  mastery is an external behavioral claim.
\end{itemize}

The total loss can rise while behavior improves because the critic is
fitting larger return targets. It can fall while the robot learns a
reward loophole. A high reward can still represent holding the ball
incorrectly, exploiting assistance, or failing after the logged horizon.
PPO only optimizes the reward and data it receives.

Therefore a basketball policy requires independent evaluation: complete
episodes, physical task metrics, deterministic exported-policy tests,
and rendered inspection. No scalar PPO metric alone establishes mastery.

\subsection{Recurrent sequence replay in
basketball}\label{recurrent-sequence-replay-in-basketball}

The basketball actor is an LSTM. Its action mean at time \(t\) depends
on:

\[
\mu_t=f(o_t,h_{t-1},c_{t-1}).
\]

If training shuffled individual time steps, the hidden state would no
longer match the one that generated the action. The basketball rollout
therefore stores:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  observations with shape \texttt{{[}time,\ environments,\ 61{]}};
\item
  sampled actions and old log probabilities;
\item
  old critic values, rewards, terminations, truncations, and bootstrap
  values;
\item
  \texttt{episode\_\allowbreak{}starts} for every time step;
\item
  the initial LSTM hidden and cell states for the rollout.
\end{itemize}

During each PPO epoch, \texttt{actor.forward\_sequence()} starts from
the saved initial hidden state and walks forward in time. Before
processing a step whose \texttt{episode\_\allowbreak{}starts} flag is
true, it multiplies the hidden and cell state by zero for that
environment. This reconstructs the recurrent state while preventing
memory from crossing resets.

The update uses the full sequence each epoch. There is no basketball
minibatch loop in \texttt{ppo\_update()}. With defaults of 32 steps and
4 environments, one rollout contains:

\[
32\times4=128\text{ transitions}.
\]

Five epochs present those same 128 transitions to the optimizer up to
five times, unless KL stopping ends earlier. This is 128 new environment
transitions, not 640 new transitions.

The critic is evaluated on a flattened
\texttt{{[}time*environment,\ 61{]}} tensor because it is feedforward.
Its outputs are reshaped back to \texttt{{[}time,\ environment{]}}.

\subsection{Feedforward minibatches in generic MLX
PPO}\label{feedforward-minibatches-in-generic-mlx-ppo}

\texttt{RolloutBuffer.reset()} allocates fixed arrays shaped
\texttt{{[}num\_steps,\ num\_envs,\ ...{]}}. It stores:

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Field
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Purpose
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{observations} & input used to choose each action \\
\texttt{next\_\allowbreak{}observat\allowbreak{}ions} & returned next
input \\
\texttt{actions} & sampled action \\
\texttt{rewards} & scalar reward \\
\texttt{terminations}, \texttt{truncations} & separate boundary types \\
\texttt{values} & old critic predictions \\
\texttt{log\_probs} & old-policy joint log probabilities \\
\texttt{advantages}, \texttt{returns} & allocated fields, although the
current PPO path passes computed arrays directly to \texttt{update()} \\
\end{longtable}
}

The generic update flattens the first two dimensions. For the library
defaults:

\[
16\text{ steps}\times4096\text{ environments}
=65{,}536\text{ samples}.
\]

With 16 minibatches, each minibatch has 4,096 samples. Two epochs
produce 32 optimizer steps per rollout.

The generic Microduck example does \textbf{not} use those large library
defaults.
\texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}examples\allowbreak{}/\allowbreak{}ppo\_\allowbreak{}microduc\allowbreak{}k.py}
defines 16 environments, 24 steps, 4 minibatches, and 5 epochs, then
passes those values into \texttt{PPOConfig}. Its rollout has 384
transitions, each minibatch has 96, and one rollout produces 20
optimizer steps.

The MLX update creates a fresh random permutation each epoch. This is
appropriate for its feedforward network because every saved observation
contains the complete input expected by the network.
\texttt{RolloutB\allowbreak{}uffer} stores no recurrent hidden state and
no sequence-start mask; using it unchanged for an LSTM would lose
necessary history.

\subsection{KL divergence: diagnostic in MLX, stopping rule in
basketball}\label{kl-divergence-diagnostic-in-mlx-stopping-rule-in-basketball}

Both implementations calculate

\[
\widehat D_{\mathrm{KL}}
=
\operatorname{mean}\left[(r-1)-\log r\right].
\]

Because samples come from the old policy, this is a common sample
approximation related to
\(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{\mathrm{old}}\|\pi_{\mathrm{new}})\). It should
be nonnegative in expectation, although a finite sample and
floating-point arithmetic can produce small irregularities.

The implementations use it differently.

\subsubsection{Generic MLX behavior}\label{generic-mlx-behavior}

\texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}rlx/\allowbreak{}algorith\allowbreak{}ms/\allowbreak{}ppo.py}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  clips log ratios to \texttt{{[}-20,20{]}} before exponentiation;
\item
  reports \texttt{approxim\allowbreak{}ate\_\allowbreak{}kl} when
  metrics are requested;
\item
  reports \texttt{clip\_\allowbreak{}fraction\allowbreak{}};
\item
  does \textbf{not} define \texttt{target\_kl};
\item
  does \textbf{not} stop epochs or minibatches based on KL.
\end{itemize}

Its KL is a diagnostic, not a gate.

\subsubsection{Basketball behavior}\label{basketball-behavior}

\texttt{ppo\_\allowbreak{}microduc\allowbreak{}k\_\allowbreak{}basketba\allowbreak{}ll.py}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  defaults \texttt{target\_kl} to \texttt{0.02};
\item
  computes KL before each epoch's optimizer step;
\item
  can stop with \texttt{epochs\_completed\ ==\ 0} if saved old log
  probabilities are already inconsistent with the current actor;
\item
  recomputes the full recurrent sequence after each optimizer step;
\item
  computes post-update KL and stops further epochs when it exceeds the
  target;
\item
  records \texttt{kl\_\allowbreak{}early\_\allowbreak{}stop}.
\end{itemize}

The pre-update guard matters for stale or mismatched rollout data. The
post-update guard limits repeated reuse of the same short recurrent
rollout. It is still not a mathematical guarantee that every state
changed by less than a fixed amount.

\subsection{Exact defaults and
overrides}\label{exact-defaults-and-overrides}

Do not describe ``the PPO defaults'' without naming the layer.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Generic \texttt{PPOConfig}
defaults}{Generic PPOConfig defaults}}\label{generic-ppoconfig-defaults}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Setting & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{num\_envs} & 4096 \\
\texttt{num\_steps} & 16 \\
\texttt{gamma} & 0.99 \\
\texttt{gae\_lambda} & 0.95 \\
\texttt{num\_\allowbreak{}minibatc\allowbreak{}hes} & 16 \\
\texttt{update\_\allowbreak{}epochs} & 2 \\
\texttt{normaliz\allowbreak{}e\_\allowbreak{}advantag\allowbreak{}es} &
true \\
\texttt{clip\_\allowbreak{}coeffici\allowbreak{}ent} & 0.2 \\
\texttt{clip\_\allowbreak{}value\_\allowbreak{}loss} & true \\
\texttt{entropy\_\allowbreak{}coeffici\allowbreak{}ent} & 0.01 \\
\texttt{value\_\allowbreak{}coeffici\allowbreak{}ent} & 0.5 \\
\texttt{max\_\allowbreak{}grad\_\allowbreak{}norm} & 0.5 \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsubsection{Generic Microduck example
overrides}\label{generic-microduck-example-overrides}

\texttt{rlx/\allowbreak{}examples\allowbreak{}/\allowbreak{}ppo\_\allowbreak{}microduc\allowbreak{}k.py}
replaces \texttt{num\_envs}, \texttt{num\_steps},
\texttt{num\_\allowbreak{}minibatc\allowbreak{}hes}, and
\texttt{update\_\allowbreak{}epochs} with \texttt{16}, \texttt{24},
\texttt{4}, and \texttt{5}. It keeps gamma \texttt{0.99}, lambda
\texttt{0.95}, clip \texttt{0.2}, and entropy coefficient \texttt{0.01}.
Its optimizer learning-rate default is \texttt{1e-3}, which belongs to
the example arguments rather than \texttt{PPOConfig}.

\subsubsection{Basketball trainer
defaults}\label{basketball-trainer-defaults}

The basketball trainer does not instantiate \texttt{PPOConfig}. Its
command line and function defaults produce:

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Setting & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
environments & 4 \\
rollout steps & 32 \\
updates & 5 \\
PPO epochs per rollout & 5 \\
gamma & 0.99 \\
GAE lambda & 0.95 \\
clip coefficient & 0.2 \\
value coefficient & 1.0 \\
entropy coefficient & 0.01 \\
gradient norm limit & 1.0 \\
Adam learning rate & \texttt{2e-5} \\
target KL & 0.02 \\
reset exploration standard deviation & 0.03 \\
\end{longtable}
}

Its environment construction also overrides training conditions
explicitly:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  episode limit: 10 seconds;
\item
  actuator: \texttt{"bam"} by default;
\item
  observation noise: off;
\item
  domain randomization: off;
\item
  pushes: off;
\item
  curriculum: off;
\item
  hold assistance: \texttt{0.0};
\item
  command: fixed \texttt{(0.08,\ 0.0,\ 0.0)} unless
  \texttt{-\allowbreak{}-\allowbreak{}randomiz\allowbreak{}ed-\allowbreak{}commands\allowbreak{}}
  is selected.
\end{itemize}

These choices describe the default local continuation experiment. They
are not universal PPO recommendations and do not reproduce the complete
upstream training recipe.

\subsection{One complete update, end to
end}\label{one-complete-update-end-to-end}

For the default basketball run:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Reset four environments with deterministic seed offsets.
\item
  Initialize LSTM hidden and cell tensors with shape
  \texttt{{[}1,\ 4,\ 256{]}}.
\item
  Collect 32 control steps, giving 128 transitions.
\item
  At every step, zero recurrent state for environments whose current
  observation starts a new episode.
\item
  Sample 14-dimensional Gaussian actions and save their joint old log
  probabilities.
\item
  Step each environment independently.
\item
  Evaluate final pre-reset observations for bootstrap values; force
  terminal bootstraps to zero.
\item
  Reset ended environments and mark the next observations as episode
  starts.
\item
  Run backward GAE with termination and truncation masks.
\item
  Normalize the 128 advantages together.
\item
  Replay the entire actor sequence from the saved initial LSTM state.
\item
  Check pre-update approximate KL.
\item
  Compute clipped policy loss, squared critic loss, and Gaussian
  entropy.
\item
  Backpropagate, clip the combined actor-and-critic gradient norm to
  1.0, and step Adam.
\item
  Replay the sequence again, measure post-update KL, and decide whether
  another epoch is allowed.
\end{enumerate}

The command-line defaults request five such rollout/update cycles:

\[
4\times32\times5=640
\text{ local environment transitions}.
\]

That small default is suitable as a short continuation or smoke-scale
run, not evidence of basketball mastery.

\subsection{Common interpretation
mistakes}\label{common-interpretation-mistakes}

\textbf{Mistake: ``The Gaussian returned probability 3.9, which is
impossible.''}\\
Correction: it returned density. Probability is area over an interval.

\textbf{Mistake: ``A positive log probability is invalid.''}\\
Correction: continuous log density may be positive when the density
exceeds 1.

\textbf{Mistake: ``Clipping keeps every ratio between 0.8 and 1.2.''}\\
Correction: the objective uses a clipped comparison. The actual ratio
can lie outside the interval.

\textbf{Mistake: ``A timeout gets zero future value because the
environment reset.''}\\
Correction: a pure time limit bootstraps from the final pre-reset
observation, then stops the trace.

\textbf{Mistake: ``Negative entropy means negative probability.''}\\
Correction: continuous differential entropy can be negative.

\textbf{Mistake: ``Lower total loss means higher reward.''}\\
Correction: total loss mixes policy, critic, and entropy terms on one
batch.

\textbf{Mistake: ``High reward means the skill is mastered.''}\\
Correction: reward is a designed training signal. Mastery requires
independent physical criteria and inspection.

\textbf{Mistake: ``The basketball updater uses MLX minibatches.''}\\
Correction: it is a separate PyTorch recurrent updater that replays full
sequences.

\textbf{Mistake: ``Both PPO implementations stop at KL 0.02.''}\\
Correction: only the basketball path has that target and early stopping.
Generic MLX PPO reports KL without stopping.

\subsection{Exercises}\label{exercises}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A Gaussian has mean 0 and standard deviation 0.2. Compute its density
  at the mean. Can the answer exceed 1?
\item
  An action has old log density \texttt{-3.0} and new log density
  \texttt{-2.8}. Compute the PPO ratio.
\item
  With advantage \texttt{+3}, ratio \texttt{1.4}, and clip coefficient
  \texttt{0.2}, compute the unclipped product, clipped product, and
  selected surrogate.
\item
  Repeat Exercise 3 with advantage \texttt{-3}.
\item
  A transition has reward 2, current value 4, final-observation value 5,
  and gamma 0.9. Compute its TD residual for a true terminal and for a
  pure timeout.
\item
  Why must an LSTM rollout save its initial hidden state and
  episode-start masks?
\item
  Under generic \texttt{PPOConfig} defaults, how many optimizer steps
  occur per rollout?
\item
  Under basketball defaults, how many new transitions are collected in
  one rollout, and how many times can they be presented to the
  optimizer?
\item
  A training log shows total loss falling and reward rising. Give two
  reasons this still does not prove basketball mastery.
\item
  State the KL behavior difference between the generic MLX and
  basketball implementations in one sentence.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Answers}\label{answers}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  At the mean, density is \(1/(0.2\sqrt{2\pi})\approx1.995\). Yes. It is
  density, and its total area is still 1.
\item
  \(r=\exp(-2.8-(-3.0))=\exp(0.2)\approx1.2214\).
\item
  Unclipped: \(1.4\times3=4.2\). Clipped ratio: 1.2. Clipped product:
  3.6. PPO selects the smaller surrogate, 3.6.
\item
  Unclipped: \(1.4\times(-3)=-4.2\). Clipped product:
  \(1.2\times(-3)=-3.6\). PPO selects \(-4.2\) because it is smaller.
  Clipping does not protect movement in the harmful direction.
\item
  Terminal: \(2-4=-2\). Timeout: \(2+0.9(5)-4=2.5\).
\item
  The same observation can produce a different action mean under a
  different hidden state. Sequence replay must reconstruct the state
  that corresponds to the stored action, and reset masks must prevent
  memory from crossing episode boundaries.
\item
  \texttt{update\_epochs\ ×\ num\_minibatches\ =\ 2\ ×\ 16\ =\ 32}
  optimizer steps.
\item
  \texttt{4\ ×\ 32\ =\ 128} new transitions. Up to five full-sequence
  optimizer presentations occur, unless KL early stopping ends the loop
  sooner.
\item
  The reward may omit important physical requirements or contain an
  exploitable shortcut; evaluation may cover only short or assisted
  episodes. In addition, loss and reward are not the same quantity.
\item
  Generic MLX PPO reports approximate KL as a metric but never stops on
  it, while basketball checks KL before and after each full-sequence
  epoch and stops further epochs when it exceeds the default target
  \texttt{0.02}.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{References}\label{references}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  John Schulman, Filip Wolski, Prafulla Dhariwal, Alec Radford, and Oleg
  Klimov. \textbf{Proximal Policy Optimization Algorithms}.
  arXiv:1707.06347, 2017. \url{https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.06347}
\item
  John Schulman, Philipp Moritz, Sergey Levine, Michael Jordan, and
  Pieter Abbeel. \textbf{High-Dimensional Continuous Control Using
  Generalized Advantage Estimation}. arXiv:1506.02438, 2015.
  \url{https://arxiv.org/abs/1506.02438}
\end{itemize}

The paper metadata and abstracts were checked against locally retrieved
arXiv pages. The repository implementation statements in this chapter
were checked separately against the local source files named at the
beginning.

\section{Basketball: Blind Recurrent Balance on a Free
Ball}\label{basketball-blind-recurrent-balance-on-a-free-ball}

\subsection{Learning objectives}\label{learning-objectives}

After this chapter, you should be able to:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  reconstruct the basketball scene from the numbers in the repository;
\item
  explain every slice of the shared 61-value observation and all 14
  actions;
\item
  describe why the actor needs recurrent memory even though the ball is
  absent from its input;
\item
  follow hidden and cell state through reset, rollout collection, PPO
  replay, ONNX export, evaluation, and Studio playback;
\item
  calculate every local reward term from the implemented formulas and
  coefficients;
\item
  explain the six-level physical-assistance curriculum without calling
  it a reward curriculum;
\item
  distinguish a released-actor warm start from a full optimizer resume
  and from training truly from scratch;
\item
  run the parser-verified training, evaluation, rendering, and
  Studio-adapter commands;
\item
  apply the physical evaluation gate and report the retained result
  honestly: \textbf{6/6 sustained balance, 0/3 commanded rolling};
\item
  state the limits: steering is not solved, lateral and yaw steering are
  not assessed, bridge behavior is not established, and no hardware
  claim is made.
\end{itemize}

only. The main sources are:

The retained artifacts were produced on September 10, 2026. The latest
Studio report evaluates seeds 101, 102, and 103 for 60 seconds under
zero and \texttt{0.15\ m/s} forward commands. All six trials sustained
balance, but none of the three commanded trials passed controlled
rolling. The adapter also states that lateral and yaw command trials are
not implemented. Therefore:

\begin{quote}
The demonstrated result is unassisted sustained balance on a free
basketball. It is not controlled rolling, general steering, bridge
traversal, climbing onto the ball, recovery from the floor, or hardware
validation.
\end{quote}

The source basketball release in
\texttt{microduc\allowbreak{}k-\allowbreak{}playgrou\allowbreak{}nd}
reports a much larger upstream evaluation, but that published evaluation
is not a result of this local trainer. This chapter keeps source
evidence, local evidence, and Studio evidence separate.
